2020-01-21 {{iX9lLFDvw_w@0}} [educational] Want big arms? Get an arm day: NH argues that maximal arm growth for naturals requires a dedicated arm day, treated as seriously as leg training. Compounds alone don't grow arms because they are secondary movers, and end-of-workout isolation is only a beginner's start. An arm day should open with an arm-friendly compound (close-grip bench, heavy rows) and progressively overload curls and extensions to heavy weights; it is low on CNS fatigue and can replace a rest day.
2020-01-21 {{tqf2di_v9KY@0}} [educational] How to spot a fake natty: rollercoaster progress: First episode of a series on recognising fake naturals without naming anyone. NH's first sign is rollercoaster progress: naturals keep most of what they build and regain it quickly via muscle memory, whereas steroid users balloon in size and strength for a few months, disappear, and return much smaller, often low in energy. He urges viewers to stay natural.

2020-01-22 {{VUNsazYt-uo@0}} [educational] How to do Coan grip: Tutorial on the Coan grip, a one-handed barbell hold from the side learned from Ed Coan. NH uses it for forearm size, not grip strength: it allows progressive overload, isn't CNS taxing, and can be supersetted with leg movements. Set safeties just above the knee, brace with obliques, hold 15 seconds per arm, once every 4-6 days.

He details his per-lift ranges (bench and squat 4-8, deadlift and overhead press 2-5, front squat always 5), his own within-range progression scheme ('864'/'543') that adds reps across sets before adding weight, and accessory ranges of 6-10, 8-12 and 10-15, with 15 reserved for four specific movements.

2020-01-24 {{P4wlPBkgzWo@0}} [educational] How to spot someone / get a good spot on the bench press (and the mistakes to avoid): NH explains why to get a spot on the bench in commercial gyms (no safeties, poorly placed J-hooks, and the risky roll of shame) and how to ask: state exactly how much help you want on the lift-off and how many reps. For spotters: listen, never jerk the bar out, apply strength gradually, be patient, use an even grip, and take the bar immediately when the lifter asks rather than forcing them to grind.
2020-01-24 {{r-H83X7QbbA@0}} [no_speech] 315 lbs (140 kg) Coan rows for 12 + 265 (120 kg) stiff legged deadlifts superset: Coan rows destroy the upper back and the stiff legged deadlifts finish the pre-taxed posterior chain.
2020-01-25 {{6JPbeLj0-xo@0}} [educational] How NOT to spot a fake natty: size/strength discrepancy: NH rejects the idea that being big but weak proves steroid use: leverages, limb length, muscle insertions and training style all affect strength. Using a thought experiment comparing weekly 1RM front squats with 5-rep sets, he argues that accumulated volume, not max strength, drives hypertrophy, and that 'strength' means something different in bodybuilding than in powerlifting.
2020-01-25 {{9c7O0jjeR5Q@0}} [educational] Serious injuries don't just "happen": NH argues that serious 'freak' injuries don't strike natural lifters out of nowhere: they come from attempting weights far beyond anything you've handled, or from overuse injuries signalled well in advance by chronic pain. He advises staying in manageable rep ranges and leaving a rep in the tank, links tendon ruptures mainly to PED use, and admits his own injuries came from ignoring warning signs.
2020-01-25 {{ZvKRTWehUDk@0}} [educational] Why you should stop wearing lifting gloves: Short video telling viewers to stop wearing lifting gloves: they add a layer that lets the bar slip despite the knurling, they smell, go spongy and need replacing. Hands adapt to the bar within about a week; NH used to wear gloves himself.
2020-01-25 {{xXS9sSvudo4@0}} [no_speech] 30 lbs armwrestling style towel curls for 8: Another armwrestling special. This one targets the forearm: lock your wrist at the top and keep the forearm tensed during the negative portion of the curl.

He illustrates with a recent low-back cramp during squats at 365 lb: he stopped the set, switched to front squats, cut back-squat reps to 4 the following sessions, used paused squats at 300 lb, and kept deadlifting because he knows the issue is his SI joint, not deadlift-related.

2020-01-26 {{4S7JYYVqIEg@0}} [educational] What shoes should you wear for lifting? (Flat sole vs high heel): NH wears heeled Olympic weightlifting shoes for all knee-flexion (squat) work and thin-soled, flat, high-ankle shoes for everything else, especially pulls from the floor. He gives two rules for buying shoes (sole as wide or wider at the toes than the heel, and a straight, not banana-curved, sole), notes that flat shoes suit low-bar squatters or very mobile people, and advises against lifting barefoot because gyms may ban it.

2020-01-27 {{8ihiO0oUZE4@0}} [educational] How to stop tearing calluses (handcare for lifters): Hand care routine for lifters to stop tearing calluses. Chalk dries the skin, so he moisturizes nightly with a climbers' balm, and he shaves calluses flat with a nail clipper every two weeks so the bar has no ridge to catch. He also shows how to trim the loose skin around a palm split so it heals faster.
2020-01-27 {{BFVw7x1fZjs@0}} [review] Captains of crush product review: Review of IronMind Captains of Crush grippers. He recommends pairing them with hand-extensor elastics to avoid elbow pain and using them during rest periods as a low-fatigue forearm accessory.

2020-01-28 {{4FY2Ct22qXU@0}} [educational] How I utilize body part splits for maximum muscle hypertrophy (versus upper lower: A defense of body part splits for natural lifters, against their bad reputation online. He argues you should group muscles that work together around one compound lift (bench, squat, deadlift), then branch out into varied accessories where most of the volume and growth happens. Days should alternate upper and lower so heavily taxed muscles recover. He finds this better than upper/lower or push/pull/legs, because those structures handle deadlifts and leg frequency badly.
2020-01-28 {{cHYOYTgiRlM@0}} [educational] How I split up my week for Natural Hypertrophy: Overview of his weekly program: an 8-day body part split (bench, squat, arms, deadlift, each in a 'competition' and a 'volume' version) run six days a week with Sundays off. He never deloads and trains 2-3 hours a day. Because the 8-day cycle runs over 6-day weeks, some main lifts drop out of a given week, which gives his body and mind a built-in break.

2020-01-29 {{UdMWt_ofY2w@0}} [educational] Why you should stop using a squat pad: Short argument against using a squat pad. The pad is bulky and can roll up the back toward the neck when you lean forward, and it makes the bar unstable and staying tight harder. The upper back discomfort without it fades within weeks. Pads are fine for hip thrusts.
2020-01-29 {{Z-D9kRwK90I@0}} [educational] Why I superset EVERYTHING (the giant sets principle): He supersets about 90% of his training as giant sets with no rest. The exception is the opening heavy compound strength work, where he rests and focuses. Giant sets save 30-40 minutes, double as cardio/GPP and don't hurt hypertrophy if pairings avoid overlapping muscles. He also uses deliberate overlap to pre-exhaust strong muscles so weak ones do the work.
2020-01-29 {{gVVj9QuCyFM@0}} [educational] How to program an arm day (the Natural Hypertrophy example): How to program an arm day, using his own two arm days as examples. An arm day opens with one heavy arm movement in a giant set that hits every target muscle, then narrows toward isolation of priority muscles. It avoids full-range-of-motion compounds and sits between taxing lower body days. Its goal is a pump and active recovery, not fatigue. He adds forearm, shoulder and upper back work and never curls below 3-4 reps.

Day 2 is snatch-grip deadlifts, deadlift-stance SSB box squats, Yates rows and rack pulls. He pulls conventional only once per cycle because frequent conventional deadlifting makes him plateau, and he aims the accessories at his weak points (lats, traps, hamstrings).

2020-01-31 {{vnssPJqYmtQ@0}} [educational] Reduce elbow pain with 0 effort in less than a minute: Lifters grip constantly (bench, squat, deadlift) but never train the opposite motion, opening the hand, and this imbalance can cause elbow pain. 2020-02-01 {{PLgCL_rizPU@0}} [educational] The training principles series： overtraining: His take on overtraining. It exists but is individual, and it shrinks as you get better, so a natural lifter should aim to become 'unovertrainable'. It is mostly a problem for eager beginners, and 'under-recovered' is a better term. Body part splits let you track how fatigued each muscle is so you can train right up to the edge without going over.
2020-02-01 {{aXjs8-XG6NU@0}} [no_speech] 185 lbs (80 kg) SSB good mornings for 12 + 160 lbs (70 kg) Coan grip superset: SSB good mornings feel amazing but are also much harder than the barbell variant. 2020-02-02 {{RaGX9rPQr4k@0}} [review] Wisconsin method aka Bulgarian light review (from a muscular hypertrophy standpo: A long critique of the Wisconsin method / Bulgarian light (work up to a daily 1RM on one lift, add weight daily, switch lifts on a stall) as promoted by Eric Bugenhagen and JJ Andersen, judged only for hypertrophy. He grants that it builds strength. His objections: too little volume/tonnage, bad for beginners, workable for few lifts, skews toward lower body PRs and an unbalanced physique, too 'lazy', drifts into unorthodox lifts, and dodges plateaus by switching lifts. It works best for one-lift obsessives, athletes, or paired with volume work elsewhere.

2020-02-03 {{TrU2m-4yZT0@0}} [educational] Is the smith machine completely useless？: He argues the Smith machine is a poor and even dangerous teaching tool for beginners. Its fixed vertical path stops the body from adjusting the bar path or saving bad form, it is easier than free weights so beginners overload it, and it doesn't train stabilizers. It is useful for advanced lifters who want to target muscles heavy without balancing (front squats, rows, sissy squats), and its square top frame makes his favorite chin-up bar.

2020-02-05 {{cknNgkOKy8Q@0}} [educational] Are you doing Romanian deadlifts wrong?: NH contrasts the popular RDL (minimal knee bend, hip hinge, stops below the knee) with the version he learned in Europe: a full-range 'reverse deadlift' starting from the rack with a slow negative to just above the floor. He explains what each offers, recommends his version for higher reps on a separate day to train the deadlift negative, and argues the popular RDL is really a stiff-legged deadlift negative.
2020-02-06 {{4IhOKe7lo_s@0}} [educational] The training principles series: aesthetics: NH defines 'aesthetics' in bodybuilding as the Apollonian physique (wide shoulders, narrow hips, V-taper, low bodyfat) and explains why his own frame (wide waist, short clavicles) can't achieve it. 2020-02-06 {{iY2uzM3vlBA@0}} [educational] The most common deadlift form mistake (fishing rod syndrome): NH describes 'fishing rod syndrome', a deadlift flaw of advanced lifters where the thoracic spine rounds with protracted shoulders and disengaged lats near lockout. He blames locking the knees too early from trying to explode off the floor, and prescribes patient, constant leg pressure so knees, hips and shoulders lock together, crediting this for four injury-free years of conventional deadlifts.

2020-02-07 {{SVGIPYo1mDg@0}} [educational] Why you should stay natural: permanent gains: NH argues the main reason to stay natural is that natural gains are permanent: natural male hormones peak later than people think, muscle built early carries into old age (his 90-year-old grandfather), and size is retained through layoffs. Muscle gained on PEDs can only be kept on PEDs; ex-users who stay big are on TRT.

2020-02-09 {{M-a4cj1yFX8@0}} [educational] The training principles series: good form: NH defines good form as technique that targets the intended muscles, keeps you safe and pain-free, and is reproducible; performance is deliberately excluded. He separates objective form rules everyone must follow (no lumbar rounding on deadlifts, no flared elbows on bench, no knee valgus) from subjective, leverage-dependent choices (hip height, knee travel, stance, elbow tuck) to tune once advanced.

2020-02-10 {{NGSCZU6obAQ@0}} [educational] The training principles series: slow negatives: Answering a subscriber, NH explains why he does slow negatives and fast positives: slower reps increase difficulty and fiber damage, preserve form, and above all improve mind-muscle connection, since the muscle you feel stretching is the one you feel working. He lists exceptions: fast but controlled bench negatives, no deadlift negatives, slow positives on curls.
2020-02-10 {{lq_qgThCqHY@0}} [educational] Having a strong grip won't give you big forearms: NH debunks the idea that a strong (static crush) grip from deadlifting builds big forearms. Forearm size depends heavily on muscle insertions; static holds are the least hypertrophic stimulus; grip strength depends on hand size and the nervous system. Forearm growth needs progressive overload across wrist and elbow flexions and all grip types, isolation work, and possibly arm-wrestling training.

2020-02-10 {{xn8RN9807Ns@0}} [educational] Should bodybuilders care about strength?: NH argues bodybuilders should care about strength, not as an end but as the most objective trainable measure of progress and a byproduct of hypertrophy. Strength exists across rep ranges and is rep-range specific, progressive overload takes many forms, and bodybuilders should train both lower and higher rep ranges because each drives the other.
2020-02-11 {{4aIHcNEDTNA@0}} [educational] Should you listen to people who don't look like they lift?: NH gives his 'trifecta' for whose fitness advice to trust: they currently demonstrate what they teach, have verifiable proof they did in the past, or have relevant credentials. Two disqualifiers override these: a record of lying or bad character, and lying about natural status. He trusts experience over theory but respects academics.

2020-02-12 {{NAkUxLa5Ttg@0}} [educational] Are compound movements important for hypertrophy?: NH argues compound movements (any multi-joint movement, not only the big three) are critical for natural bodybuilders: they save time, let you move the most total weight per session, recover better between sets and warm up muscles for follow-up isolation work in a body-part split. He wants to reclaim the big three from powerlifting and says deadlifts save him hours of posterior-chain work.
2020-02-12 {{VAblRcVMjqw@0}} [educational] What you should NOT be doing during a bulk: NH recounts bulking from 120 to 240 lbs over 7-8 years: the 120-190 phase was nearly textbook, but chasing weightlifting strength he overate to 240 lbs at 6 ft and got fat, realizing it only after breaking his ribs squatting. He advises a surplus no bigger than 10-15%, tracking calories or eating instinctively while monitoring the scale, and near-maintenance lean gaining over dirty bulking.
2020-02-13 {{3qbZ-gpkOmw@0}} [educational] What really happens when you injure your shoulder: NH explains that shoulder injuries are almost always structural (rotator cuff or AC joint), not muscle tears, citing a non-English study where only 2 of 400 injured lifters had a muscle (front-delt) tear, both from kipping pull-ups. Shoulder vulnerability comes from rotation limiting safe range of motion, as with behind-the-neck pressing.
2020-02-13 {{CB2_PT6IhxY@0}} [lift_clip] Push ups + isometric hold + Meadows raises superset: Clip of an upper-body pump superset (push-ups, isometric pull-up hold, Meadows raises) filmed during a snowstorm, set to French rap music. 2020-02-13 {{aiVrEt5ziHQ@0}} [educational] Do you need to lift heavy to get big?: NH says 'lift heavy to get big' is true but incomplete: leverages matter more than muscle size for strength, and hypertrophy comes from tonnage (total weight moved). 'Heavy' is personal and rep-dependent; train within roughly 60-90% of max (about 2-15 controlled reps), avoid junk volume at both extremes, and rotate rep schemes.

2020-02-19 {{Ox6TQAC9cW0@0}} [educational] The training principles series: frame & flow: Defines 'frame' (the skeleton: height, clavicle width, waist, hips, joint size, limb length) and 'flow' (how the whole physique ties together aesthetically). Small joints make muscles look bigger; flow hinges mostly on a tight core and balanced limbs. A bodybuilder should assess their frame objectively and pack muscle in the right places to create a finished work of art.

2020-02-20 {{eU_f4lGi9vA@0}} [rant] Stop using a mixed grip: Rant against the mixed (over/under) deadlift grip for anyone but competing powerlifters who can't hook grip. It risks biceps tears, creates rotation, trap and lower back asymmetries that spill into other lifts, and distracts from deadlift form. Use double overhand with straps (or hook grip) instead.

(theoretical aspects): Standing demonstration of what a small frame means, using his own short clavicles and narrow shoulders and back. Small frames look thicker but lack natural width; rather than chase the wide look, prioritise the muscles around the outline (triceps long head, medial/rear delts, traps) and limb development to create the illusion of width.

2020-02-22 {{uTHCzF7Exck@0}} [review] Transcendent training review (from an hypertrophy standpoint): Reviews 'transcendent training' (taking a 20-rep weight up to a single 100-rep set, resting only while holding the weight). 2020-02-25 {{QiOh8SF-UxQ@0}} [educational] Why you should leave reps in the tank (Reps in Reserve): Explains his strategy of always leaving reps in reserve on strength work: stopping short of grindy reps keeps form perfect, protects joints, preserves energy for the volume work that drives hypertrophy, and nearly eliminates injury risk. He contrasts the Western grind-to-failure mentality with the Eastern school's habit of repeating a weight until it's easy, and argues that slow, injury-free progress wins in the long run.

2020-02-28 {{d7441LfRS9s@0}} [educational] The training principles series: noob gains: Defines 'noob gains' as the rapid strength and muscle gains of the first months of lifting, explains why they happen (novel stress makes muscles rebuild bigger), and argues beginners should use that grace period to learn compounds and some isolation to set up their long-term career, since gains later become a game of inches for naturals.

He uses his own fast-growing triceps long head as proof, rejects a fixed natural limit, and wonders why former athletes seem to keep growing longer.

2020-02-29 {{y_lPAdWi4sU@0}} [educational] When are you intermediate and what it means programming wise (for bodybuilding): Defines an intermediate in bodybuilding not by strength numbers but by knowledge (compounds, variations, some isolation) and mindset: the moment you start tinkering with your own program toward a clear physique goal. Intermediates should raise volume and frequency, tailor exercises to their goals, train within a 70-90% intensity window, and start managing diet, sleep, alcohol and stress. He claims most lifters quit at the novice-to-intermediate transition, blaming rushed powerlifting novice programs, and argues bodybuilders should stay novices longer.

2020-03-09 {{KMcJAERb9Dk@0}} [educational] Response to Steve Shaw (Massive Iron) - Going to WAR Over FRONT DELT training: In a respectful response video to Steve Shaw (Massive Iron), NH argues that front delt isolation (front raises, especially cables) is not the cause of shoulder problems; high-frequency horizontal pressing is. He contends that a strong, even dominant, muscle protects its joint, that imbalance means undertrained antagonists rather than an overdeveloped muscle, and that raises offer a different, joint-friendly stimulus. He agrees front delts are the one muscle that needs isolation least and that rear and medial delts deserve priority, but says what should be dropped for bad shoulders is excessive horizontal pressing, not front raises.

2020-03-14 {{1roZlKwps8Y@0}} [educational] How to avoid bicep tears: Explains where biceps tears happen and how to prevent them. 2020-03-15 {{8s56W2sjc2g@0}} [review] Should you buy this instead of resistance bands? (SPRI review): Compares SPRI handled resistance tubes with looped resistance bands. Bands are better for accommodating resistance on a barbell; SPRI tubes are better for direct exercises like curls, overhead press and pull-aparts because the rotating handles spare the wrists. He advises buying the lightest tube first and notes that bands freeze in very cold garage gyms while SPRI tubes don't.

2020-03-21 {{dpwvdQqKxQI@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: volume vs tonnage: First episode of the hypertrophy series, defining terms. Volume is sets x reps regardless of weight; tonnage is the total weight moved, and tonnage is what NH cares about most for growth. Hard sets are the bodybuilder's bread and butter, junk volume exists, and tonnage is best counted from working sets only, though warm-ups still matter. He uses 'tonnage' as a bodybuilding-specific term.

2020-03-23 {{RxnaWqkkVlg@0}} [educational] Building a home gym in a cold environment: Five considerations for a garage or shed gym in an arctic or poorly insulated location: experience a winter before building, don't trust insulation, don't count on heaters to fight outside cold, be honest about your cold tolerance, and expect frozen barbells and bands. 2020-03-23 {{g4N7gpzHm5k@0}} [educational] "Isolation movements are useless because you can't really isolate a muscle": Rebuts the minimalist claim that isolation exercises are useless because true isolation is impossible. Isolation movements prioritise a muscle even if others assist, let you bias regions (e.g. biceps short head) through grip and angle, and allow focused, less taxing work, whereas compounds are a shotgun that let dominant muscles take over. Hypertrophy programs without isolation produce imbalances; don't make a training method your religion.
2020-03-24 {{Jd2wr0Qe42I@0}} [educational] Raw garlic for insane pumps & a stronger immune system: NH explains why he eats raw garlic daily: it's believed to boost immunity (though not a coronavirus cure), and in his experience it improves pumps and vascularity. He covers downsides (breath and body odour, blood thinning at very high doses), dosage (start with one clove, ramp up, 3-6 cloves/day, never more than half a bulb), crushing or slicing it so it releases its compounds, and buying organic.

The caveat: adding PEDs turns it back into a dangerous addiction.

2020-03-25 {{ggFrswc8T5E@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: mentally vs physically taxing exercises: Second hypertrophy-series episode introducing two programming categories: physically taxing exercises (big compounds, especially legs, which drain the cardiovascular system and need longer recovery) and mentally taxing ones (heavy, scary lifts). Both are individual: for NH, high-bar squats and overhead press are the most taxing while deadlifts and bench feel easy. Programs should spread taxing days and account for mental recovery.
2020-03-26 {{_iaU9_rM1QQ@0}} [educational] Can lift variations/accessories affect your form on the main lifts?: NH discusses whether variations and accessories can change technique on the main lifts. Many variations (deficit deadlifts, pause squats, pause bench, front squats) help form, but some (stiff-leg deadlifts, good mornings, box squats) can reinforce bad patterns, especially if they fully replace the main lift. Keep training the main lift and pick variations that target your individual weak points.

2020-04-09 {{IgxJijW3MSQ@0}} [educational] Weak point Wednesday: Trapezius: NH covers trap growth for naturals. Insertions matter (his small clavicles make his traps look big), and naturals can build impressive traps even though traps respond strongly to androgens. 2020-04-09 {{tZ9CSu2DMIE@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: maximum recoverable volume: NH defines maximum recoverable volume (MRV) as the most volume you can recover and improve from. He argues an extra set within a session rarely hurts; the real risk is hitting the same muscle again too soon, so he gives each muscle two days. MRV is individual, found by experimentation, and should rise over time, driving ever-increasing tonnage. Most people undertrain from fear of overtraining, and strength gains without more tonnage won't build size.
2020-04-10 {{37WbiLy4Dkc@0}} [rant] You don't need science to see results: NH argues you can make big gains without scientific knowledge, as he did in his first five years, because the body is the teacher. Beyond a basic threshold (technique and form from basic creators like Omar Isuf and Alan Thrall), more literature adds little. He criticises science-obsessed fitness for producing timid, smug lifters who over-control variables and under-work, and says his channel is experience-based.

2020-04-14 {{-wk3jSpm434@0}} [educational] The training principles series: full range of motion: NH explains that range of motion depends on leverages and that 'full' ROM standards are subjective conventions mainly useful for judging strength sports. For bodybuilding, full ROM stretches the muscle more and prepares you for deep reps, so do it about 80% of the time, but partials have a place if chosen for gains, not ego. Avoid excessive ROM (hyperextending curls, butt wink) and adapt deadlift start height if needed.

2020-04-16 {{uf3-n8CFeu0@0}} [educational] My favorite ab isolation exercises: He shows two quick, stretch-focused ab isolation finishers done at the end of a workout: a straight-leg crunch alternating band-assisted and bodyweight reps (upper abs/obliques), and a dumbbell-between-the-feet lying leg raise alternating loaded and bodyweight reps (lower abs). 2020-04-17 {{7Ax5O5DKoUg@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: How to balance mentally taxing exercises in your training: He explains mental fatigue as a recovery factor: some lifts (for him the squat) spike stress and drain confidence, while total volume also drains focus. Within a session, start with the stressful compound but avoid a declining 'staircase' that makes you skip late isolation work; place stress 'spikes' later to stay engaged. Across the week, never stack mentally taxing days back to back, and use prioritization to decide which big lift gets the emphasis.
2020-04-18 {{yenqGI_FVQ4@0}} [physique_update] 10 Year Natural Transformation 15-25 (from 110 to 210 lbs): 1000-subscriber special walking through photos from age 15 to 25: from a 110 lb high-schooler doing knee push-ups, to 6 ft 140 lb at 18, through bedroom dumbbell training with daily biceps, to gym training, a clean bulk to 210 lb, then an over-aggressive bulk to 235 lb at ~21-22% bodyfat and a crash cut. Downsides: slippery under the feet for squats, presses and bench leg drive, they feel like they steal drive energy, gather humidity, shift and crease, and don't fully protect against repeated deadlift drops, so he recommends two layers or plywood underneath.

He doesn't use RPE or know his 1RM. The key point: volume only builds muscle inside the right intensity window, where the target muscle, not cardio or mechanics, is what fails.

2020-04-29 {{ADLnarIVojI@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: muscular vs cardiovascular fatigue: He distinguishes muscular fatigue (fiber micro-damage that reduces force output and drives hypertrophy through recovery) from cardiovascular fatigue (being out of breath), which many 'fatigue-seeking' YouTube programs mistake for an effective stimulus. The goal is enough damage without injury, around maximum recoverable volume, with enough recovery time; soreness is a poor recovery indicator.

Warm-up needs are individual and affect programming, and time saved matters because naturals need so much volume.

2020-05-09 {{Vw9MhD5Amfw@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: the hypertrophy rep range conundrum: NH argues that no single hypertrophy rep range like 8-12 exists; any rep range grows muscle if it brings enough stress. The practical range is whatever reaches maximum recoverable volume efficiently, safely and quickly, which rules out very heavy singles and very high-rep light work as staples. Varying rep ranges, like varying implements, is itself a form of intensity variety.

2020-05-20 {{Vbk_2AALm3M@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: strength baseline control for recovery & gains: Introduces the 'baseline': your ability to reproduce the same performance (same weight, same reps) after exertion. It exists between sessions (recovery, which dictates frequency and heavy/light days) and within a session. Quality sets are those that break your ability to return to baseline within the workout; sets you can repeat endlessly (e.g. easy push-ups, 5x5 with equal reps across sets) accumulate lower-quality tonnage, except in nucleus overload where repeatability is the point.

Evolving rep ranges are the built-in tool for this: a heavy low-rep set lowers tonnage now but builds the strength for multiple higher-rep sets later. Flexible programs raise tonnage best.
2020-05-30 {{KTuKLbl0TOE@0}} [educational] The training principles series: specificity: He argues the strength-sport principle of specificity applies to bodybuilding too. Pick compound lifts that suit you, then surround them with close variations that carry over, because lifting is a neurological skill and constantly novel exercises keep you relearning and lower your tonnage. Odd exercises are fine as exceptions, never as the rule.

He recommends tight evolving rep ranges that keep roughly the same total reps.

2020-07-03 {{zyjCsFRlBT0@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: artificial vs organical failure: NH distinguishes organic from artificial failure. Artificial muscular failure is stopping because of lactic acid burn before the fibers are truly fatigued, avoided by using rep ranges heavy enough that the burn arrives at or after the end of the set. Artificial mechanical failure is unintended form breakdown, while organic mechanical failure is a deliberately disadvantaged position, like a crane-style stiff-leg deadlift, used to target a muscle.

2020-07-09 {{8I6IPBr2_z0@0}} [educational] The training principles series: synergy: NH defines synergy, an overarching principle above carryover and specificity: every lift in a program should be fed by others. Synergy remains subject to volume, intensity and frequency.

These break monotony, reduce overuse injuries and strengthen weak positions, while tonnage stays stable enough to keep progressing.

EZ bar cheat curls turn into supinated cleans driven by technique and hips and stress the shoulder, so negatives are better done on a preacher bench. Dumbbell cheat curls, done alternating with weight in both hands and starting from a neutral grip, force a real curl finish and are his preferred method.

2020-09-03 {{rIngKQqTJMs@0}} [educational] Lower back rounding: The Good, the Bad and the Ugly: A case-by-case argument on lower and upper back rounding in squats and deadlifts, built off Geoffrey Verity Schofield's video. Rounding is neither absolutely dangerous nor absolutely safe: risk only exists for a specific load, lift and individual. The real trap is thoracic rounding, because the spine gives up in stages and thoracic rounding drags the lumbar spine into rounding. For bodybuilders, chasing extra load by rounding is self-defeating anyway: it shifts work away from the legs and costs gains.

2020-09-12 {{K-iofHQxDsA@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: intensity windows and their applications: Introduction to intensity windows: intensity is simply the difficulty of a lift expressed as %1RM or RPE, it is the most underrated programming pillar, and most training should fall between 60 and 95%. He explains how relative intensity rises with fatigue, how deliberately 'wrong' percentages can predict fatigue later in a session, and that you can find working weights by reps without knowing your 1RM.

2020-10-24 {{Rz4uzjM068M@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: utilizing back-off sets to accumulate quality tonnage: NH defines back-off sets as challenging sets in a relevant intensity window after strength work, not light finishers, and describes three approaches: same lift, a close variation, or his own speed back-offs. 2020-10-29 {{iEKSZ806mrc@0}} [educational] Should you lock out your lifts?: NH argues that lockout and depth standards come from competition judging, not hypertrophy. Lockout is where the skeleton holds the load and muscles are least engaged, so for bodybuilding it adds little and repeated locking can wear joints. Lock out only when you can do so painlessly every week. Full range at the bottom matters for injury prevention, while lockout strength never needs training.
2020-10-29 {{ylbxNE-2Im0@0}} [educational] Assessing your ancestry/DNA to hone in your diet: NH explains how he shaped his diet around the diseases that run in his family (colon and prostate) by talking with male elders, with guidance from a longevity nutritionist, rather than genetic tests. He urges viewers to investigate what their relatives died of, protect their genetically weak organs, cut foods that disagree with them even subtly, and favor foods that slowly make them feel better.
2020-10-30 {{Lj0m82JUtkM@0}} [review] 4 Days Minimalistic Upper body Dominant PPL (Push x2) program review: NH reviews a viewer's 4-day push/pull/push/legs minimalist program and largely approves it. He moves the deadlift to pull day as the opener and barbell rows to leg day with higher reps, suggests supersets and a second push-day variation, and lists missing work such as calves, rear delts, biceps and long-head triceps.
2020-10-31 {{4uTKFMZVgSs@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: absolute vs relative intensity: NH defines absolute intensity as your fresh, warmed-up ability to move weight, which changes only slowly with strength progress, and relative intensity as the declining capacity during a session. Strength work uses absolute intensity at the start, while the rest happens in relative intensity windows. Junk volume is sets that fall outside a relevant window measured against absolute intensity, not relative.
2020-10-31 {{fluJhyCvQQQ@0}} [no_speech] Abs finisher: The great thing about abs is that they don't take that long to work. Often times, what truly limits you is not your muscular endurance but rather your ability to conquer the pain that stems from it.
This set is something I like to do at the end of a lower body day, when the core is already fatigued from all the compound movements and responds well to repeated stretches and contractions

2020-11-02 {{S8T3hQgsrko@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: understanding sandbagging to promote qualitative sets: NH defines sandbagging as doing less than you could in a set without a good reason, usually forced by rep schemes like 10x10, 5x5 or 3x5. Intentional sandbagging is useful in warm-ups and as one rep in reserve on strength work. Back-off work should go to failure. Late-session sets in irrelevant intensity windows become a form of sandbagging even at failure.

2020-11-04 {{YtNUZjBxdtE@0}} [educational] How to avoid hamstring pulls during deadlifts: NH argues that hamstring pulls on deadlifts happen because the deadlift trains hamstrings only in the positive, and many lifters never train their eccentric lengthening. 2020-11-06 {{-1dECrWYeFk@0}} [educational] Why your strength potential might be limiting your size gains: NH explains that strength does not equal size, although well-programmed long-term strength progression does bring size. They should train for reps, variation and tonnage instead.
2020-11-07 {{A-asa7QMiG0@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: programming strength work to bolster tonnage quality & progression: NH defines strength work as the session-opening, highest-intensity work done with absolute intensity and present every day. It should be synergistic with the rest of the session, ideally a big compound lift, limited to 1-3 sets with one rep in reserve. It gives the highest-quality but minority share of tonnage, warms you up for what follows, and sets the baseline for judging later sets.

It is a short block borrowed from weightlifting that patches missing volume rather than a progressable rep scheme.

2020-12-17 {{QLUXXk0OtX4@0}} [educational] Using the lower back as a bridge (the new lift with your legs): NH argues the lower back should act as a rigid bridge between the upper body (which holds the bar) and the legs (which move it) in squats and deadlifts. Any lumbar flexion is form breakdown; keeping the spine neutral lets the legs be the limiting factor, prevents overuse injuries and allows him to squat and deadlift heavy twice a week each.

2021-01-02 {{0YrBT05ZwYM@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: the inevitability of frequency control for optimal progression: NH argues that frequency is as important as volume and intensity, the 'holy trinity' of programming, which can't be separated. For naturals it's the most neglected lever and the one left when volume and intensity stall. Spreading the same work across the week beats cramming it into one day, and legs and especially upper back respond to high frequency.

2021-01-27 {{bTin_tBGoVo@0}} [educational] Why Novices shouldn't do Box Squats: NH argues that box squats are not an easier or better substitute for back squats for novices. The box gives you something to push off, a square box forces a sit-back, wide-stance, good-morning pattern, it skips the hardest part of the squat, and it does not build mobility. He suggests turning the box into a triangle and recommends box front squats as the one useful variant.

2021-01-28 {{dyeeKOQJhrM@0}} [educational] The Specificity Tree Explained (a complete guide to muscle specialization): NH lays out his tree of specificity metaphor. Lifting is a tree, each movement pattern is a branch, and the top lift of each branch is your priority. Tonnage falls like rain from the top lifts downward, and neighbouring branches carry over to each other. He uses it to organise exercise selection, prioritisation, recovery and supersets.

2021-02-06 {{AeIKc3A2FiY@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: pairing neurological adaptation and muscle gains: NH explains neurological adaptation, which he calls technical points. It is the learned ability to perform and recruit muscles in a lift, and it caps out after weeks to months. After that, strength gains reflect muscle (or mass) gains. Technique also regresses without practice, so keep each pattern trained at least weekly, using frequency and close variations rather than volume.

2021-02-12 {{p-kR3RfGBHw@0}} [educational] The Recovery Pot Explained (a complete guide to muscle recuperation): NH explains recovery as a pot that holds muscle damage. Its size sets maximum recoverable volume, it drains at a rate set by how fast you recover, and volume, intensity and frequency decide how much goes in, how fast and how often. He argues the goal is to keep the pot near full between medium and maximum recoverable volume, that each muscle has its own pot, that an empty pot wastes gains, and that once-a-week bro splits are therefore ineffective.

2021-02-18 {{hDAcqWHzrJE@0}} [educational] 5 Reasons why Bro Splits are Garbage: NH gives five reasons natural lifters should avoid bro splits (one body part per day, each trained once a week). 2021-02-20 {{gjZ6qGRxS10@0}} [educational] The hypertrophy series: why using metabolic stress for muscle growth fails: NH argues that the pump comes from fast reps with light weight rather than from muscle damage. Training for it leads to mechanical failure (acid, blood) instead of muscular failure, which makes it junk volume. Most growth comes from heavy, intense sets taken to muscular failure, and a set that can be repeated almost identically a minute later was not damaging.

2021-04-12 {{YKCNJS5KNvw@0}} [educational] Fasting for bodybuilding & how to maintain and make gains during Ramadan/Lent: NH explains that fasting works for fat loss only because it builds a calorie deficit, that muscle loss during fasting is overblown, and that fasting's real value is spiritual and for controlling appetite. 2021-04-15 {{C86TMVYGu0M@0}} [educational] Pushing progression and avoiding plateaus using evolving rep ranges: NH explains why evolving rep ranges push plateaus away better than static ones: you can repeat a weight while total tonnage keeps rising. 2021-07-06 {{ZRycM7TrC_8@0}} [no_speech] 440 lbs (200 kg) snatch grip deadlifts for 4: That last rep is a good example of what I consider to be bad form on the deadlift: over-utilization of the lower back, under-utilization of the legs. 2021-07-16 {{AFK9x4EZh2Q@0}} [educational] Top 3 most underrated exercises: NH names his three most underrated movements: the lying pullover, the properly performed dumbbell row, and the bench-supported 'Arnold raise' for side and rear delts. All are sustainable weekly for life with low injury risk and target the V-taper, upper back and round shoulders most people want but rarely program.
2021-07-18 {{3bEkmyM8NBQ@0}} [educational] How to squat to maximize muscle growth and minimize injury risks: NH gives squat pointers rarely covered elsewhere: initiate with knee flexion rather than a hip hinge to avoid turning the squat into a good morning, narrow the grip progressively to keep the upper back packed, low bar for tall lifters, a four-step walkout, finishing reps before re-racking, a controlled positive, and a lying glute-flex trick to find foot angle.
2021-07-20 {{-U6X37OXuLA@0}} [educational] ANIME WORKOUTS - Can you look like Adam from Record of Ragnarok in real life?: NH designs two time-efficient, low-impact programs to build Adam's slim 'swimmer's physique' from Record of Ragnarok: a 2-day weights template done twice a week and a 4-sessions-per-week bodyweight template. Both emphasize shoulders, lats, chest, arms, forearms, abs and calves, leave legs mostly untrained for athletes' sport work, and suit beginners or people who don't want to get big.
2021-07-21 {{GH8tqzGTgH8@0}} [rant] The form p*lice is under arrest: Hour-long rant against the online 'form police', the commenters who correct other people's lifting form without being asked. NH argues that form depends on the individual's leverages, that hypertrophy depends on the distance travelled under tension rather than end positions (ATG depth, lockout, top squeeze), and that the form police ignore real form errors like stiff-leg RDLs, nose-level overhead presses and wall-backed curls. He closes by attacking the opposite anti-form dogmatism as well and argues for a logical middle ground.

One flag can be tolerated, two call for caution, and three mean you should unsubscribe.

2021-08-27 {{Q5y4wM8zcWY@0}} [educational] Why Novices shouldn't do Barbell Rows: NH argues that bent-over barbell rows (and Pendlay rows) are misprogrammed: they are a hip-hinge/horizontal-pull hybrid that taxes the lower back, compete with squats and deadlifts, and get cheated with the legs, so novices should skip them. Novice programs include rows for their deadlift carryover, not for upper back growth. He recommends pull-ups for novices, then rows in a rotation with deadlifts or as strict upper-back work, with dumbbell rows as the best variation.

2021-09-02 {{yvhW-zyhpsM@0}} [educational] Good leg day vs Bad leg day: NH contrasts the typical long, isolation-heavy, once-a-week leg day with a short minimalist session: open with a heavy knee flexion, follow with a hip hinge such as RDLs, then finish with isolation in giant sets. He argues for training legs at least twice a week, treats heavy soreness as a sign of low frequency, and uses the spare room on leg day for abs, calves, neck and other small muscles.

2021-09-13 {{zXqHX-uxTU8@0}} [other] How to lead a happy life (The Educated Barbarian: Seneca analysis, part. 4): Part 4 of NH's reading of Seneca's On the Happy Life, covering the chapters on the force of precepts and on peace of conscience. 2021-09-15 {{PYICG0hBlVo@0}} [educational] Why you NEED a goal body: NH argues that every lifter should actively build a personal 'goal body' and visualise it constantly. It works as a distant absolute target, the star, paired with realistic yearly sub-goals, the ladder. The goal must be your own, ideally based on fictional figures rather than real people, and must have no hard deadline, to avoid the 'Arnold effect' of beginners quitting over impossible targets.
2021-09-17 {{XncJr9uMpWo@0}} [educational] Want to save some time AND grow your muscles? Supersets are your best friends: NH explains why every program he writes, novice programs included, uses supersets. Pairing non-competing muscles cuts rest time without hurting performance. He covers how to start (bi-sets), how rest works (none inside the superset, 3-4 minutes after), keeping strength work out of supersets, and limits on giant sets.
2021-09-18 {{twEeL-z4Zug@0}} [story] Don't make this mistake: NH tells how his deadlift stalled at 485 lb for months because he slowly started bending over the bar more and pulling with a rounded lower back. He didn't notice until he saw his PR footage, then denied it out of ego. Going back to his old upright technique and intention got him an immediate 495x2 PR. His lesson: poor form is a choice, so film yourself and stay honest.

2021-10-08 {{QxwtSRAYGjw@0}} [educational] Want big & healthy biceps? Try elastic curls: NH demonstrates band curls with a light band anchored low on a rack as a finisher that keeps the biceps tendon healthy through full extension, which his heavy partial curls avoid. 2021-10-10 {{OWbmOI3cuNs@0}} [review] Better than 90% of bodybuilding routines: 4 HORSEMEN by Brian Alsruhe: NH reviews strongman Brian Alsruhe's 4 Horsemen giant-set program, praising its self-regulation, compact sessions and how it teaches lifters to program. 2021-10-13 {{I2aR7F5rCuE@0}} [educational] Samurai Lifting Wisdom: NH draws lessons for lifters from Miyamoto Musashi's Book of Five Rings: train for life, do not hasten the blooming of the flower, become your own teacher, and build knowledge from small things. 2021-10-14 {{_Ak93cVwd1c@0}} [educational] Can you look like an Olympic weightlifter naturally?: NH examines whether a natural can achieve the physique of an Olympic weightlifter. He argues the snatch and clean and jerk are among the worst lifts for hypertrophy (explosive, no eccentric, low reps, highly technical) and that lifters' looks come partly from drugs and are a byproduct of performance. The look is achievable naturally by 'dehybridizing' the lifts into their component patterns or 'bastardizing' them (power/hang/block variations, snatch-grip pulls).
2021-10-17 {{THV7B11oalk@0}} [review] The Best/Worst of Reddit Fitness - The Ten Commandments of Lifting: NH reviews a top r/fitness post listing ten 'commandments' of lifting, finding most of it decent if obvious, with some pseudo-scientific myths (cardio causing insulin resistance, post-workout window, no protein/fat after training). He argues the list over-focuses on nutrition and gimmicks while barely mentioning training, which is the true priority.

2021-10-20 {{f-XiVNy25tw@0}} [educational] Level up your abs: NH explains why abs dominate fitness culture (Hollywood, attractiveness), argues a developed core is the single most important aesthetic area because it ties upper and lower body together, and debunks bad-ab-genetics, oblique and 'abs are made in the kitchen' myths. His method: brace hard on compounds, then superset stretch-focused ab exercises daily with low intensity and moderate-high volume.

He argues that strength and hypertrophy are different pursuits: bodybuilding is about building quality tonnage across many lifts. 2023-03-20 {{-izcpmcGs-c@0}} [educational] Stop falling for these Ab Training Myths: First in a myth-busting series: NH attacks 'abs are made in the kitchen', the idea that compounds and bracing build abs, the claim that sit-ups hurt the back, the EMG-based dismissal of leg raises, and the fear that ab and oblique training thickens the waist. 2023-03-24 {{1apseBbt0-o@0}} [educational] What everyone gets WRONG about Range of Motion: A lecture on range of motion within technique (load, ROM, tempo). NH argues there is no universally optimal ROM; ROM only matters together with load and biomechanics, so the target is 'effective ROM', the part of the movement that puts the target muscle under the most stress. He criticises dogmatic full-ROM and lockout obsession (often rooted in powerlifting and weightlifting rules) and shows how trimming useless ROM on pullovers, lateral raises and RDLs improves load, reps and stimulus-to-fatigue, with tempo applied last.
2023-03-26 {{iPtxiNtBY-M@0}} [educational] The ULTIMATE Forearm Exercises Tier-List (Popeye-tier to Skeletor-tier): NH ranks about 50 forearm exercises, tools and sports from Skeletor (D) to Popeye (S) tier, judging each by whether it targets a forearm function (wrist flexion/extension, pronation/supination, elbow flexion, isometric grip) without taxing the rest of the body. 2023-05-09 {{7KDB5wzgY4c@0}} [educational] Come with me if you want ABS! (6 PACK MASSterplan): A three-step abs plan. Step 1 is one stretch-based exercise (sit-ups) for 6-9 sets a week, unweighted for max reps. Step 2 keeps the volume the same and adds weight within an 8-12 or 10-15 rep range. 2023-05-17 {{Zqv7KVsmu78@0}} [educational] Training for Aesthetics: You're doing it WRONG.: Programming is only half the job; execution is the other half. He covers three things. Tempo: pick one controlled negative speed and keep it on every rep, warm-ups included, to justify the load, not to chase time under tension. Form: it must be repeatable and controlled, and a set ends when form breaks. Load: heavier only helps if the lift, technique and tempo are right.

He then gives and ranks the 10 metrics he uses to judge a lift for hypertrophy: enjoyment first, then progression consistency, active range of motion, ease of use, stimulus-to-fatigue ratio, stability, joints, cardio impact, comfort and soreness. He ends by calling for critical thinking about the hidden value systems of fitness influencers.

(Program Review LIVE): A three-hour live stream where NH reviews about 15 viewer programs one after another, between chat questions on training, diet, cardio and his own lifting. The same corrections keep coming up: too little or badly spread back volume, rep ranges that are too wide or too high, redundant exercises, badly ordered supersets, and bodyweight pull-ups that should be weighted.

Drawing on Debord's Society of the Spectacle, he argues fitness has become something people watch rather than do, and that real change needs a year-round habit, physically earned confidence and a personal 'why'.

His keys were hypertrophy-friendly compounds (heel-elevated squats on a slant board, RDLs, narrow good mornings, hyperextensions), heavy stable split squats as a main lift, reducing leg frequency to twice a week with low volume, and adding hard cardio and weighted carries; isolation is useful but he hasn't needed it.

2025-03-15 {{ACFKXT44BzM@0}} [educational] New Hypertrophy Gimmick™ just dropped 🤡: NH critiques the low-rep (4-6/4-8, 'anything above 8 is junk volume') hypertrophy method popularized by Paul Carter. He grants that low reps suit heavy compounds and weighted calisthenics and can reduce fatigue, but argues narrow low rep ranges stall progression on isolation lifts, fail with rest-pause, push people toward machines only, and amount to a softer repackaging of Mentzer's HIT. He works across 3-15 reps and returns to YouTube after a long break.
